\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+...

(84)

\displaystyle=\left[\gamma\tau-\gamma^{2}\lambda\sigma{R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)+\gamma^{3}\lambda\sigma\kappa\left({R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)\right)^{2}-\gamma^{4}c\sigma\kappa^{2}\left({R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)\right)^{3}+\cdots\right]{\delta}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1})

(85)

This series can be resummed using the geometric series, in field theory often referred to as Dyson summation
Le Bellac 1991
. Rearranging the sum gives

\displaystyle\tau_{R}(\omega_{1})

\displaystyle=\left[\gamma\tau+\gamma\frac{\lambda\sigma}{\kappa}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\left(-\gamma\kappa{R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)\right)^{r}\right]

(86)

\displaystyle=\left[\gamma\tau+\gamma\frac{\lambda\sigma}{\kappa}\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\left(-\gamma\kappa{R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)\right)^{r}-\gamma\frac{c\sigma}{\kappa}\right]

(87)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{\gamma\tau}{1+\gamma\kappa{R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)}+\gamma\left(\underbrace{\tau-\frac{c\sigma}{\kappa}}_{=0}\right)\right]

(88)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma\tau}{1+\gamma\kappa{R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)}

(89)

where we made use of the bare values given in Eq. (
14
), i.e., replaced
\sigma
with
\tau
and used
\lambda=\kappa
at bare level. This vertex interpolates the physical pictures for
\gamma=0
, where no deposition takes place (
\tau_{R}=0
), and
\gamma\to\infty
, where every newly visited site gets marked immediately by a deposited trace. For
\gamma\to\infty
, the coupling tends to

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to\infty}\tau_{R}(\omega_{1})=\frac{n_{0}}{{R}\left(x,\omega_{1}+i\varepsilon\right)},

(90)

using
n_{0}=\tau/\kappa
.

Appendix E
Derivation of four non-Markovian correction terms to trace function

When expanding the nonlinear action in Eq. (
72
) to all orders in
\gamma
and to
\mathcal{O}(g^{2})
, the nonvanishing contribution of joint perturbative order
\gamma^{n}g^{2}
is obtained by inserting the (Fourier-transformed)
g^{2}
-decoration

y_{1},\tilde{\omega}_{1}
y_{2},\tilde{\omega}_{2}
y_{3},\tilde{\omega}_{3}
y_{4},\tilde{\omega}_{4}

(91)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}{\tilde{\omega}_{1}}\ldots{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}{\tilde{\omega}_{4}}{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,...{\rm d}{y_{4}}\,\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{1},\tilde{\omega}_{1})\partial_{y_{2}}\varphi(y_{2},\tilde{\omega}_{2})C_{2}(\tilde{\omega}_{3}+\tilde{\omega}_{4})\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{3},\tilde{\omega}_{3})\partial_{y_{4}}\varphi(y_{4},\tilde{\omega}_{4})\times{\delta}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(\tilde{\omega}_{1}+\tilde{\omega}_{2}+\tilde{\omega}_{3}+\tilde{\omega}_{4})

into the expansion of the trace function
\left\langle\psi\widetilde{\varphi}\right\rangle
as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{Q}(x_{0},x,\omega){\delta}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega+\omega^{\prime})

(92)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{2+n}\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\omega_{1}}...{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\omega_{n}}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}_{1}}...{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}_{4}}{\rm d}{z_{1}}\,...{\rm d}{z_{n}}\,{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,...{\rm d}{y_{4}}\,

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi(x,\omega)\left(-\lambda\widetilde{\psi}(z_{1},\omega_{1})\varphi(z_{1},\omega_{1})\psi(z_{1},\omega_{1})\right)\left(-\kappa\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{2},\omega_{2})\widetilde{\psi}(z_{2},\omega_{2})\varphi(z_{2},\omega_{2})\psi(z_{2},\omega_{2})\right)\right.

\displaystyle...\left(-\kappa\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{n-1},\omega_{n-1})\widetilde{\psi}(z_{n-1},\omega_{n-1})\varphi(z_{n-1},\omega_{n-1})\psi(z_{n-1},\omega_{n-1})\right)\left(\sigma\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{n},\omega_{n})\widetilde{\psi}(z_{n},\omega_{n})\varphi(z_{n},\omega_{n})\right)\varphi(x_{0},\omega^{\prime})

\displaystyle\times\left.g^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{1},\tilde{\omega}_{1})\partial_{y_{2}}\varphi(y_{2},\tilde{\omega}_{2})C_{2}(\tilde{\omega}_{3}+\tilde{\omega}_{4})\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{3},\tilde{\omega}_{3})\partial_{y_{4}}\varphi(y_{4},\tilde{\omega}_{4})\times{\delta}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(\tilde{\omega}_{1}+\tilde{\omega}_{2}+\tilde{\omega}_{3}+\tilde{\omega}_{4})\right\rangle.

As usual, we employ Wick’s theorem to evaluate this average over Gaussian random variables: When expanding the average
n_{0}^{-1}\left\langle\psi(x,\omega)\widetilde{\varphi}(x_{0},\omega^{\prime})\right\rangle
in powers of
\gamma
and
g
, as shown in Eq. (
92
), the resulting coefficients are averages over finite products of
\varphi_{1},...\varphi_{j_{1}},\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}...\varphi_{j_{2}},\psi_{1}...\psi_{j_{3}}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}..\widetilde{\psi}_{j_{4}}
fields (where
\varphi_{i}=\varphi(z_{i},\omega_{i})
etc.) with respect to the Gaussian measure (defined by the action in Eq. (
11
) for
\gamma=0
). Each of those coefficients is evaluated using Wick’s theorem, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi_{1}...\psi_{j_{1}}\widetilde{\psi}_{1}...\widetilde{\psi}_{j_{2}}\varphi_{1}...\varphi_{j_{3}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}...\widetilde{\varphi}_{j_{4}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S};\gamma=0}=\sum_{\text{pairings}}\prod_{(k_{m},\ell_{m})}\left\langle\phi_{k_{m}}\phi_{\ell_{m}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S};\gamma=0}

(93)

where the sum runs over all possible pairwise pairings of the
j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}
indices, and we use
\phi
in lieu of
\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}
to alleviate notation. Although the number of possible combinations of such pairings is very large, the right hand side of Eq. (
93
) drastically simplifies because most of the pairwise averages vanish under the Gaussian average.
Again, as in the case of
g=0
(cf. App.
B
), the only non-vanishing Gaussian correlators are those of the form
\left\langle\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}\right\rangle,\left\langle\psi\widetilde{\psi}\right\rangle
as given by Eqs. (
75
), (
76
).
Thus, the sum over averages in Eq. (
92
) simplifies into a sum over integrals over products of these two Gaussian propagators. The integrals run over
n
internal spatial variables
z_{1},...,z_{n}
which stem from the expansion in
\gamma^{n}
, and four internal spatial variables
y_{1},...,y_{4}
which stem from the expansion in
g^{2}
(and thus are related to the non-Markovian correction). Analagously, the integration also runs over
n
frequencies
\omega_{1},...,\omega_{n}
stemming from the
\gamma^{n}
-expansion and four frequencies
\tilde{\omega}_{1},...,\tilde{\omega}_{4}
from the
g^{2}
-expansion.
The integral over the internal space variables
z_{1},...,z_{n}
, simplifies significantly, since the corresponding
\left\langle\psi\widetilde{\psi}\right\rangle
propagators (cf. Eq. (
76
)) are proportional to spatial
\delta
-functions.
This results in all internal space coordinates to identify as
z_{1}=...=z_{n}=x
.

The integral over the internal spatial coordinates
y_{1},...,y_{4}
appearing in the four
\varphi
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
fields in Eq. (
92
), however, is less trivial as it involves the correlators (“propagators”) of
\left\langle\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}\right\rangle
. Likewise, the integration over
\tilde{\omega}_{1},...,\tilde{\omega}_{4}
, further involves the correlator of the driving noise,
C_{2}(\tilde{\omega}_{3}+\tilde{\omega}_{4})
. Hence, the integrals stemming from the
g^{2}
-expansion need to be dealt with more carefully:
The four fields
\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{1},\tilde{\omega}_{1})\partial_{y_{2}}\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{2},\tilde{\omega}_{2})\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{3},\tilde{\omega}_{4})\partial_{y_{4}}\varphi(y_{4},\tilde{\omega}_{4})
appearing in every term of Eq. (
92
) have to be paired up (according to Eq. (
93
)) with another creation field
\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{i})
or annihilation field
\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{i})
appearing in Eq. (
92
), respectively, in order to have a non-vanishing contribution (cf. Eq. (
75
)). Up to permutation of indices, each of these possible combinations (which we refer to as “
Wick pairings
”, Eq. (
93
)) can fundamentally be grouped into four different ways that are best understood diagrammatically:
In any case, each of the two
\varphi(y_{i})\partial_{y_{j}}\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{j})
attaches via Wick-pairing to a
\varphi(z_{k})\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{\ell})
field appearing in the terms of Eq. (
92
). Concurrently,
\widetilde{\varphi}(z_{k}),\varphi(z_{\ell})
, Wick-pair with two other corresponding fields giving rise to connected
\left\langle\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}\right\rangle
- “propagators” and so on. Thereby, the Wick-pairing of
\varphi(y_{i})\partial_{y_{j}}\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{j})
, diagrammatically speaking, splits an existing propagator in the
\gamma^{n}
-expansion into two or, as shown below, in three.

In the expansion to order
\gamma^{n}
, there is one propagator from
x_{0}
to
x
(
transition
) and
n
propagators from
x
to
x
(
return
) represented by loop diagrams. The
g
-vertex can thus occur in four different ways, Fig.
2
, to be distinguished by whether and how the
g
-vertex enters into the initial
transition propagator
,
,
in

\tilde{\omega}

\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{II}}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\partial_{y_{2}}

(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x,y_{1},\omega\right)\right){T}\left(y_{1},x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\frac{1}{{R}\left(x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(x,y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})\ ,

\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{II}}(x_{0},x,\omega)\\

\times\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\!\big(\gamma

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right){T}\left(y_{1},x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\frac{\gamma(-\kappa)}{1+\gamma\kappa{R}\left(x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(x,y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 29.53 0 C 29.53 5.44 25.12 9.84 19.69 9.84 C 14.25 9.84 9.84 5.44 9.84 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 33.46 -3.94 L 33.46 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 -3.94 L 13.78 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right]

(113)

\displaystyle=n_{0}^{-1}\lim_{\gamma\to\infty}\left[\gamma\frac{1}{-i\omega}\frac{(-\lambda)\gamma}{1+\gamma\kappa R(x,\omega)}\right.

(114)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\times\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-i\omega}\frac{1}{{R}\left(x,\omega\right)}\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

(115)

The trace function corrected to leading order in the external noise is thus given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(x_{0},x,\omega)=\frac{{T}\left(x_{0},x,\omega\right)}{(-i\omega){R}\left(x,\omega\right)}+g^{2}\left[\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{I}}+\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{II}}+\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{III}}+\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})

(116)

Comparing the four correction terms,
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{I}},\ldots,\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{IV}}
, it turns out that they draw on two different integrals,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

(117)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\frac{\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right){T}\left(y_{1},x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}{{R}\left(x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(x,y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

(118)

with
\mathcal{J}_{1}
entering into
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{I}}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{IV}}
, Eqs. (
96
) and (
115
), and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
entering into
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{II}}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{III}}
, Eqs. (
106
) and (
111
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{I}}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{-i\omega}\frac{{T}\left(x_{0},x,\omega\right)}{\left({R}\left(x,\omega\right)\right)^{2}}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,x,\omega)

(119)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{II}}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-i\omega}\frac{{T}\left(x_{0},x,\omega\right)}{\left({R}\left(x,\omega\right)\right)^{2}}\mathcal{J}_{2}(x,x,\omega)

(120)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{III}}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{-i\omega}\frac{1}{{R}\left(x,\omega\right)}\mathcal{J}_{2}(x_{0},x,\omega)

(121)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{IV}}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-i\omega}\frac{1}{{R}\left(x,\omega\right)}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega).

(122)

Eq. (
116
) simplifies further when factorising out the term to order
g^{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(x_{0},x,\omega)=\frac{{T}\left(x_{0},x,\omega\right)}{(-i\omega){R}\left(x,\omega\right)}\left[1+g^{2}\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)-\mathcal{J}_{2}(x_{0},x,\omega)}{{T}\left(x_{0},x,\omega\right)}-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,x,\omega)-\mathcal{J}_{2}(x,x,\omega)}{{R}\left(x,\omega\right)}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})

(123)

To simplify notation, we introduce

\displaystyle T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)-\mathcal{J}_{2}(x_{0},x,\omega)

(124)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left[\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right)-\frac{{T}\left(y_{1},x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}{{R}\left(x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(x,y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right)\right]

(125)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right){T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)\right)

(126)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\left[\frac{{T}\left(y_{1},x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}{{R}\left(x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)}{T}\left(x,y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)-{T}\left(y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}\right)\right]C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

where the last equality follows by integration by parts and re-arranging terms, arriving at Eq. (
29
).
Using
T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)
in Eq. (
123
) it may be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{T(x_{0},x,\omega)+g^{2}T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)}{(-i\omega)\left({R}\left(x,\omega\right)+g^{2}T^{(2)}\left({x,x,\omega}\right)\right)}+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})\ .

(127)

In keeping with the notation of
T^{(2)}
as the
g^{2}
-correction to the transition probability, we henceforth write
T^{(0)}
for what used to be called
T
, the contribution at
g=0
. Collecting these terms into the
renormalised

T
, we write

T(x_{0},x,\omega)=T^{(0)}(x_{0},x,\omega)+g^{2}T^{(2)}(x_{0},x,\omega)+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})

(128)

and along the same lines the return probability

R(x,\omega)=T(x,x,\omega)=R^{(0)}(x,\omega)+g^{2}R^{(2)}(x,\omega)+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})=T^{(0)}(x,x,\omega)+g^{2}T^{(2)}(x,x,\omega)+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})\ ,

(129)

so that

\mathcal{Q}(x_{0},x,\omega)=\frac{T(x_{0},x,\omega)}{(-i\omega)R(x,\omega)}+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})\ .

(130)

Appendix F
Derivation of effective transition probability for Brownian Motion driven by self-correlated noise

To compute
T^{(2)}\left({x,\omega}\right)
in Eq. (
126
),
we firstly express the Fourier-transformed correlation function
\hat{C}_{2}(\omega)
in terms of the inverse Laplace transform
\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)
, using

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{2}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}{t}\,e^{i\omega t}C(|t|)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\rm d}{t}\,e^{i\omega t}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,e^{-\beta|t|}\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)

(131)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{t}\,\cos(\omega t)e^{-\beta t}\right)\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)

(132)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\frac{2\beta}{\omega^{2}+\beta^{2}}\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\ ,

(133)

which facilitates the calculation of the convolution over
\tilde{\omega}
in the second line of Eq. (
126
), in particular when we consider exponential correlations, Eq. (
35
), in which case
\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\propto\delta(\beta-\beta^{*})
. We first consider the convolution of
C_{2}
with
T^{(0)}
,

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\omega}\,T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}}\right)C_{2}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\omega}T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}}\right)\frac{2\beta\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)}{\tilde{\omega}^{2}+\beta^{2}}

(134)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)\ ,

(135)

where we have used that
T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega}\right)
cannot have any poles in the upper half-plane, because its inverse Fourier transform
T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\tau}\right)
must vanish for all
\tau<0
. If there were any poles in the upper half-plane, the auxiliary path that for
\tau<0
must pass through the upper half plane would enclose them, producing
T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\tau}\right)\neq 0
.

Considering secondly the convolution of
C_{2}
with the term of the form
T^{(0)}T^{(0)}/R^{(0)}
in Eq. (
126
), we similarly obtain

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\frac{T\left({y_{1},x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}}\right)}{R\left({x,\omega-\tilde{\omega}}\right)}T\left({x,y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}}\right)C_{2}(\tilde{\omega})

\displaystyle=\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{\tilde{\omega}}\chi_{\text{FPT}}^{(0)}(y_{1},x_{1},\omega-\tilde{\omega})T\left({x_{1},y_{2},\omega-\tilde{\omega}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\frac{2\beta\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)}{\tilde{\omega}^{2}+\beta^{2}}

(136)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\chi_{\text{FPT}}^{(0)}(y_{1},x_{1},\omega+i\beta)T\left({x_{1},y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)

(137)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\frac{T\left({y_{1},x,\omega+i\beta}\right)}{R\left({x,\omega+i\beta}\right)}T\left({x_{1},y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)

(138)

where we made use of the Markovian formula, Eqs. (
60
) and (
6
),

\chi_{\text{FPT}}^{(0)}(\omega)=T\left({x_{0},x;\omega}\right)/R\left({x;\omega}\right)
, which is, like
T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega}\right)
above, a Fourier transform of a probability density that vanishes for all
\tau<0
and thus has no poles in the upper half-plane.

Having performed the convolutions over
\tilde{\omega}
, turning them into easier integrals over
\beta
, what remains are the two spatial integrals over
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
,

T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,\left[\frac{T\left({y_{1},x,\omega+i\beta}\right)}{R\left({x,\omega+i\beta}\right)}T\left({x,y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)-T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)\right]\\
\times\left(\partial_{y_{1}}T\left({x_{0},y_{1},\omega}\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}T\left({y_{2},x,\omega}\right)\right)

(139)

We proceed by calculating
T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)
for the particular case of Brownian Motion, which has transition propagator

\displaystyle T\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)=\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{k}\frac{e^{ik(x-x_{0})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}k^{2}}=\frac{e^{-|x-x_{0}|\sqrt{\frac{-i\omega}{D_{x}}}}}{\sqrt{-4i\omega D_{x}}}\ .

(140)

Beginning with the simpler integrand in Eq. (
139
), the first term we consider is

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,T\left({y_{1},y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)\left(\partial_{y_{1}}T\left({x_{0},y_{1},\omega}\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}{T}\left(y_{2},x,\omega\right)\right)

(141)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{k}\,{\rm d}{p}\,{\rm d}{q}\,\frac{e^{ik(y_{2}-y_{1})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}k^{2}+\beta}\frac{(ip)e^{ip(y_{1}-x_{0})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}p^{2}}\frac{(-iq)e^{iq(x-y_{1})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}q^{2}}

(142)

Integration over both
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
results in two delta functions
{\delta}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(k-p)
and
{\delta}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(p-q)
, respectively. Integrating over both
{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{k}
and
{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{q}
then results in

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{p}\frac{p^{2}e^{ip(x_{1}-x_{0})}}{\left(-i\omega+D_{x}p^{2}\right)^{2}\left(-i\omega+D_{x}p^{2}+\beta\right)}\ ,

(143)

which using partial fractions can be expressed in terms of the Markovian transition densities (
cf

(
Walter et al. 2021
, Eq. (125))
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{p}\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\frac{p^{2}e^{ip(x_{1}-x_{0})}}{\left(-i\omega+Dp^{2}\right)^{2}}-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\frac{p^{2}e^{ip(x_{1}-x_{0})}}{-i\omega+Dp^{2}}+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\frac{p^{2}e^{ip(x_{1}-x_{0})}}{-i\omega+Dp^{2}+\beta}\right)

(144)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\beta^{-2}(\partial_{x_{1}}^{2})\left[-i\partial_{\omega}\beta T\left({x_{0},x_{1},\omega}\right)-T\left({x_{0},x_{1},\omega}\right)+T\left({x_{0},x_{1},\omega+i\beta}\right)\right]\ .

(145)

Expressing
T\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)
as a Fourier-transform in
t
, further leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int{\rm d}{t}\,e^{i\omega t}\beta^{-2}\left[\beta t-1+e^{-\beta t}\right](\partial_{x_{1}}^{2})T\left({x_{0},x_{1},t}\right)

(146)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int{\rm d}{t}\,e^{i\omega t}t^{2}Y\left(\beta t\right)(\partial_{x_{1}}^{2})T\left({x_{0},x,t}\right)

(147)

where we introduced the dimensionless scaling function

\displaystyle Y(z)=\frac{e^{-z}-1+z}{z^{2}}

(148)

with
Y(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle z\to 0}}{{\rightarrow}}1/2
and
Y(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle z\gg 1}}{{\simeq}}z^{-1}
.
While
Y(z)
is specific to Brownian Motion, other stochastic processes give rise to similar scaling functions as
(
Walter et al. 2021
, Eq. (97))
indicates for the case of an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process.

The second contribution of Eq. (
139
) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}(x_{0},x,\omega)

(149)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,\frac{T\left({y_{1},x,\omega+i\beta}\right)}{R\left({x,\omega+i\beta}\right)}T\left({x,y_{2},\omega+i\beta}\right)\left(\partial_{y_{1}}{T}\left(x_{0},y_{1},\omega\right)\right)\left(\partial_{y_{2}}T\left({y_{2},x,\omega}\right)\right)

(150)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\sqrt{4D_{x}(\beta-i\omega)}\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}{y_{1}}\,{\rm d}{y_{2}}\,\int\!\!\!\!\!\int{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{k_{1}}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{k_{2}}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{p}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{q}

(151)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\frac{e^{ik_{1}(x_{1}-y_{1})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}k_{1}^{2}+\beta}\frac{e^{ik_{2}(y_{2}-x_{1})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}k_{2}^{2}+\beta}\frac{(ip)e^{ip(y_{1}-x_{0})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}p^{2}}\frac{(-iq)e^{iq(x-y_{2})}}{-i\omega+D_{x}q^{2}}\ ,

using
1/R\left({x,\omega+i\beta}\right)=1/T\left({x,x,\omega+i\beta}\right)=\sqrt{4D_{x}(\beta-i\omega)}
, Eq. (
140
).
Integrating over
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
produces two
\delta
-functions,
\delta(p-k_{1})\delta(q-k_{2})
. Using them when integrating over
k_{1},k_{2}
gives

\mathcal{I}_{2}(x_{0},x,\omega)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\sqrt{4D_{x}(\beta-i\omega)}\\
\times\left(\int{\rm d}{q}\,\frac{(-iq)}{(-i\omega+D_{x}q^{2})(-i\omega+D_{x}q^{2}+\beta)}\right)\left(\int{\rm d}{p}\,\frac{(ip)e^{ip(x_{1}-x_{0})}}{(-i\omega+D_{x}p^{2})(-i\omega+D_{x}p^{2}+\beta)}\right)

(152)

By symmetry, the integral over
{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt{q}
vanishes and thus
\mathcal{I}_{2}=0
.
For Brownian Motion, we thus obtain for
T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)
, Eqs. (
29
) and (
126
),

\displaystyle T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,\omega}\right)=-\mathcal{I}_{1}(x_{0},x,\omega)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)\int{\rm d}{t}\,e^{i\omega t}t^{2}Y\left(\beta t\right)(\partial_{x_{1}}^{2})T\left({x_{0},x,t}\right)

(153)

By inverting the Fourier transform we further obtain

\displaystyle T^{(2)}\left({x_{0},x,t}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)t^{2}Y\left(\beta t\right)(\partial_{x_{1}}^{2})T\left({x_{0},x,t}\right)

(154)

\displaystyle=\Upsilon(t)(\partial_{x_{1}}^{2})T\left({x_{0},x_{1},t}\right)

(155)

where we introduced the time-stretch function, Eq. (
31
),

\displaystyle\Upsilon(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)t^{2}Y(\beta t)

(156)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\frac{\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}\left[e^{-\beta t}-1+\beta t\right]\ .

(157)

Making use of the properties of the Laplace transform, we find

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\beta^{-2}e^{-\beta t}\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{s}\,sC_{2}(t+s)

(158)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\beta^{-2}\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{s}\,sC_{2}(s)

(159)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{\beta}\,\beta^{-1}t\bar{C}_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{s}\,tC_{2}(s)

(160)

and after some simple transformations,

\displaystyle\Upsilon(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}{s}\,\Big(sC_{2}(t+s)-sC_{2}(s)+tC_{2}(s)\Big)=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}{s}\,(t-s)C_{2}(s)=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}{s}\,\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}{u}\,C_{2}(u)\ ,

(161)

as in Eq. (
31
).

Appendix G
List of explicit results for visit probabilities

The concrete perturbative corrections resulting from formulas (
28
) and (
29
) are often cumbersome expressions. The correction for the active thermal Brownian Motion on a real line, characterised by (
30
), and with an exponentially correlated driving noise is given implictly via Eq. (
34
).
This integral can be performed using Mathematica
Wolfram Research, Inc. 2021
and delivers

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(x,\omega)=\frac{e^{-\sqrt{\frac{-i\omega x^{2}}{D_{x}}}}}{-i\omega}\left[1+\frac{g^{2}D_{y}}{D_{x}^{2}\beta}\left(D_{x}\sqrt{(-i\omega)(\beta-i\omega)}\left(e^{-\frac{\sqrt{\beta-i\omega}-\sqrt{-i\omega}}{\sqrt{D_{x}}}|x|}-1\right)+\frac{1}{2}\beta\sqrt{-iD_{x}\omega}|x|\right)\right]

(162)

In the joint limit of
\beta\to 0
and
D_{y}\beta=w^{2}
fixed, the moment generating function becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(x,\omega)=\frac{e^{-\sqrt{\frac{-i\omega x^{2}}{D_{x}}}}}{-i\omega}\left[1+\frac{g^{2}w^{2}}{8D_{x}^{2}}\left(x^{2}-\sqrt{\frac{D_{x}x^{2}}{-i\omega}}\right)\right]

(163)

This corresponds to the visit probability of a Brownian motion with a random but fixed additional drift term
y
which is Gaussian distributed with mean zero and variance
w^{2}
.

In
Walter et al. 2021
, we developed a perturbative framework which was able to compute the moment-generating functions of first-passage time distributions for processes of the form (
21
). This framework did not use field theory, but instead a functional perturbation theory. Since
\mathcal{Q}(x,\omega)=\frac{1}{-i\omega}\chi_{\text{FPT}}(x,\omega)
, we here report the findings for two other models first reported there, for future reference.

First, we report the visit probability of an active Brownian Motion on a ring of radius
r
, hence

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{t}=\xi_{t}+gy_{t}\qquad x_{t}\equiv x_{t}+2\pi r.

(164)

We then study the visit probability over a certain angle
\theta=\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{r}
.
As a shorthand, we further introduce the inverse diffusive timescale
\alpha^{-1}=r^{2}/D_{x}
. To leading perturbative order, the visit probability is then given by
(
Walter et al. 2021
, Eq. (127))

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\theta,\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh\left((\theta-\pi)\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}{(-i\omega)\cosh\left(\pi\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}

(165)

\displaystyle+\frac{D_{y}g^{2}}{2D_{x}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\tanh\left(\pi\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}{-i\alpha^{-1}\beta\omega}\left[\frac{\cosh\left((\theta-\pi)\sqrt{\alpha^{-1}(\beta-i\omega)}\right)}{\sinh\left(\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{-1}(\beta-i\omega)}\right)}2\sqrt{\alpha^{-1}(\beta-i\omega)}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{\cosh\left((\theta-\pi)\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}{\cosh\left(\pi\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}\left(\pi\bar{\beta}-2\sqrt{\alpha^{-1}(\beta-i\omega)}\coth\left(\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{-1}(\beta-i\omega)}\right)\right)+\frac{\sinh\left((\theta-\pi)\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}{\sinh\left(\pi\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}\bar{\beta}\left(\pi-\theta\right)\right]

In the limit of infinite radius,
r\to\infty
, one finds
\theta\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\to\sqrt{-i\frac{\omega(x_{1}-x_{0})^{2}}{D_{x}}}
, and accordingly the Markovian result converges, as expected to

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\cosh\left((\theta-\pi)\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}{(-i\omega)\cosh\left(\pi\sqrt{-i\alpha^{-1}\omega}\right)}=\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{(-i\omega)}\frac{\cosh\left(\sqrt{-i\frac{\omega}{D_{x}}}|x_{1}-x_{0}|-\sqrt{-i\frac{\omega}{D_{x}}}\pi r\right)}{\cosh(\sqrt{-i\frac{\omega}{D_{x}}}\pi r)}=\frac{e^{\sqrt{-i\frac{\omega}{D_{x}}}|x_{1}-x_{0}|}}{-i\omega}

(166)

Analagously, a more involved computation using, for instance, Mathematica confirms that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathcal{Q}^{\text{ring}}(\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{r},\omega)=\mathcal{Q}^{\text{line}}(x_{1}-x_{0},\omega)

(167)

with
\mathcal{Q}^{\text{ring}}(\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{r},\omega)
being the result in Eq. (
165
) and
\mathcal{Q}^{\text{line}}(x_{1}-x_{0},\omega)
the perturbative result found in Eq. (
162
).

Finally we consider the case of a harmonic trap, i.e.,

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{t}=-\alpha x+\xi_{t}+gy_{t}.

(168)

which we refer to as active thermal Ornstein Uhlenbeck process (ATOU). The result is more compactly given in dimensionless units

\displaystyle\bar{\beta}=\alpha^{-1}\beta\qquad\bar{x}_{0}=x_{0}/\ell\qquad\bar{x}_{1}=x_{1}/\ell\qquad\text{with}\qquad\ell=\sqrt{D_{x}\alpha^{-1}}

(169)

The Fourier transformed visit probability is given in terms of parabolic cylinder functions
D_{-\nu}(x)
(
Gradshteyn and Ryzhik 2007
) and reads (for
x_{0}<x_{1})

(
Walter et al. 2021
, Eq. (100))

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(x,\omega)=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{\bar{x}_{0}^{2}-\bar{x}_{1}^{2}}{4}}\frac{D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(\bar{x}_{0})}{(-i\omega)D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(\bar{x}_{1})}

(170)

\displaystyle+\frac{g^{2}D_{y}\bar{\beta}}{D_{x}(-i\omega)}\frac{(-i\alpha^{-1}\omega)e^{\frac{{\bar{x}_{0}}^{2}-{\bar{x}_{1}}^{2}}{4}}}{2\left({\bar{\beta}}^{2}-1\right)D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{1}){}^{2}D_{-{\bar{\beta}}+i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{1})}

\displaystyle\times\left[\left({\bar{\beta}}+1\right)(-i\alpha^{-1}\omega+1)D_{-{\bar{\beta}}+i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{1})\left(D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{0})D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega-2}(-\bar{x}_{1})-D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega-2}(-\bar{x}_{0})D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{1})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-2({\bar{\beta}}-i\alpha^{-1}\omega)D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega-1}(-\bar{x}_{1})\left(D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{0})D_{-{\bar{\beta}}+i\alpha^{-1}\omega-1}(-\bar{x}_{1})-D_{-{\bar{\beta}}+i\alpha^{-1}\omega-1}(-\bar{x}_{0})D_{i\alpha^{-1}\omega}(-\bar{x}_{1})\right)\right]
